ParlaMint-DK_20210517100025	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister for Social Affairs and the elderly. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517100039	Thank you for that, President. With the motion for a resolution, the proposers want to require the government to establish by 2021 a new type of exit plan which provides real protection for vulnerable citizens, such as: homeless people who are being pressured by gangs to commit crime. The proposers want the new type of exit plan to take into account that the homeless and socially vulnerable cannot be included in the current exit programmes targeted at bikers and gang criminals. The current exits run under the so-called national framework model and are targeted individuals who are registered or have been registered as affiliated with a rocker gang-grouping in Denmark. In addition, persons who are not or have been registered as a gang member but who, on a specific assessment, have or have had an association with the biker and gang environment may be in the target group for an exit. Thus vulnerable homeless people exploited by the environment are not a direct target group for an exit. At the same time, I understand that the proposers are addressing the problem and I am pleased that we share the desire to help particularly vulnerable people such as homeless people, who often live a life from day to day and who are particularly vulnerable to being exploited by the gangs, perhaps because they have an abuse that can cause severe withdrawal, or perhaps because the mental illness is allowed to rage in periods without treatment. I myself visited Odense Municipality to hear about the problem and how they deal with it. Here they stated that there was good cooperation with both the local civil society and the Fyn Police, and that among other things they were: is concerned about how to help vulnerable citizens across the municipal border. Of course, it is quite unacceptable for gangs to push society's weakest to commit crime. As a society, we have a special responsibility to look after them. Having said that, however, the government cannot support the proposal. We cannot do this because the authors of the motion neither indicate nor consider how or in what context the initiative is to be financed. The government has a clear and strong ambition to strengthen its efforts towards the most vulnerable in our society. Today we do not have a safety net that is sufficiently finely masked. Therefore, we are not always good enough to seize the most vulnerable people who, because of drug abuse or mental illness, can be easy victims of the brutal behaviour of criminals. We must strengthen the fight against homelessness, in which, despite good intentions and initiatives, we have more than 650 million. kr. Since 2009 there has been an increase in the number of homeless people. We must and must do something about this, and it requires a significantly different approach from what we have done in the past across changing governments. These are discussions that we will be addressing shortly when the parties behind the reserve are presented with the study on the spread of housing first for which funds were set aside in the reserve agreement for 2020. These are discussions I look forward to, because we have set ourselves the task of doing better in Denmark when it comes to homelessness. At the same time, we in the government are working for better and more coherent help for people with double strain. We must ensure that it is not the exposed themselves who have to adapt to the logic of the system that you first have to be treated for a drug use before you can be cared for and treated for a mental illness • and vice versa • and where too many of the most vulnerable end up as throwing balls and therefore never get the help they need. We must also strengthen the seeking abuse treatment and ensure that people with abuse receive the substitution treatment they need. Psychiatry and social psychiatry have been under pressure for many years, for many people have not received sufficient help with great consequences for their quality of life. We have therefore started work on a 10-year plan for psychiatry, which must end with many years of delay and must set a long-term direction for the area in general. That is why I also welcome it if, in appropriate contexts, the proposers want to give priority to concrete initiatives that improve conditions for the socially vulnerable and that can help solve the problem of vulnerable citizens being pressured by criminal gangs to commit crime. It can, for example, be done. be in the discussions on how to strengthen efforts in one of the areas I have just mentioned, or in the context of discussions on police action against the exploitation of vulnerable citizens by gangs. We on the part of the government, as I said, agree with the intentions behind the proposal, which is why I would also like to invite the proposers to come forward with proposals for concrete solutions and concrete proposals on how to finance one or more initiatives that will put an end to the gangs pushing society's very vaguest to commit crime. Thank you for the floor for now.

They don't have a door to close behind or a hot bed to lie in, they only have themselves and the street. It's a tough life, and criminal gangs certainly don't make it any easier. Violence, threats and economic blackmail are some of the best practices used by gang criminals against our most vulnerable citizens and homeless people. Unfortunately, it is everyday that our vulnerable and homeless citizens are forced to sell drugs and they are threatened with life and limb if they refuse. The worst is in Odense, where almost one in five vulnerable citizens have felt threatened by gang members. In several places this has resulted in vulnerable and homeless citizens ending up in a vicious spiral of debt to the gangs, which they have not even sought out themselves. We simply do not think we can turn a blind eye to this. The resolution here is about establishing a new type of exit plan to protect vulnerable citizens who are being pressured by gangs to commit crime. The motion for a resolution calls for the exit plan to include a follow-up programme to help vulnerable and homeless citizens out of possible abuses, focusing on creating personal development for the individual citizen. In addition, the proposers propose that witness protection be possible so that citizens can feel safe when giving evidence. Finally, the proposers stress that the exit plan should allow citizens to make a real start so that they can put behind them all the problematic parts of their old lives. In the Left, we fully agree that there is a need for a new exit plan for vulnerable and homeless citizens who are being pressured by gangs to commit crime. We cannot tolerate vulnerable and homeless citizens in this way being harassed and subjected to violence and threats on the lives of gangs that they have not themselves sought out. In the Left, it is our fundamental view that we as a society have a responsibility to extend the necessary help and support to those citizens who are most at risk. We have a special obligation to the very weakest so that they can have the right help and real opportunity to improve their lives. Left and then government helped put an end to gang crime with the three gang packages. Together, they have resulted in increased penalties for a number of serious crimes such as serious violence, witness threats and violations of the arms law, as well as increased penalties for systematic or organised blackmail. Unfortunately, it is just that criminal gang members are inventive creatures, and it has turned out that they are now also exploiting vulnerable and homeless citizens to continue their criminal deeds. It is a constantly tough environment, which means that vulnerable and homeless and abusers remain victims in the gang environment, because gang criminals are precisely at the forefront of the illegal drug market. We should not accept that. That is why we in the Left also think that this resolution is a good one, because it must provide an opportunity for the vulnerable and homeless, who are being pushed by the gangs, to have a real new opportunity for a new start to life. Against this background, we in the Left can support this proposal. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517103421	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister for Social Affairs and the elderly. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517103431	Thank you for that, President. I'm gonna have to get the talking desk back down. You're relatively tall, Mrs. Britt Bager. I hadn't thought about that. Or maybe I'm the one who's relatively low if we're gonna be honest. In B 238, it is proposed that the government should submit by the end of 2021 a draft law on the incorporation of the UN Disability Convention into Danish law. The motion for a resolution seeks to ensure a greater obligation to take account of the rights of people with disabilities and to clarify their ability to invoke the Convention independently and independently in the courts. It also wants to send a positive signal both nationally and internationally that Denmark will strengthen the rights of people with disabilities on an equal footing with other Western countries. Finally, it is emphasised that Danish incorporation will have great national and international importance, as people with disabilities continue to be oppressed in many countries and struggle to be heard and have their rights respected. It is almost 12 years since Denmark, with the ratification of the Disability Convention, confirmed the fundamental rights of people with disabilities. A strong signal was sent nationally and internationally that Denmark will actively protect and promote these rights. The signal was only sealed with the accession of the SR government to the optional protocol on redress to the UN Disability Committee in 2014. Now we write 2021, and what do we see if we take stock of the rights of people with disabilities in Denmark? Progress has been made: the ban on discrimination outside the labour market due to disability entered into force in 2018; the right to fair individualisation in daily offers, the primary school, free schools and private primary schools was introduced earlier this year; and last month a majority in the Folketing increased the penalty for hate crimes against people with disabilities. However, we are not in the finish line. In Denmark, people with disabilities do not always receive the necessary support to live the life they want. A large group of people with disabilities are outside the labour market. Years of problems in the specialized social area have left too many without the help and support of our community. This has contributed to a lack of confidence and concern for legal certainty in this area. It must be possible to live as normal a life as possible with a disability regardless of residence, and no matter which family you were born into. But today, too many people experience it as a struggle to get the right specialized help. This is due, among other things, to the loss of knowledge which the municipal reform brought back in 2007 and which the organisations in the field of disability feared at the time. Together with the contract paper parties, that is, our support parties, we set a new direction for Denmark after the elections; a fairer direction to ensure that people with disabilities can get the help they need to participate in the community and realise their dreams. With what our objective is, then, the question: why do we not allow the incorporation of the disability convention to be one of the steps that will ensure this direction? The Disability Convention contains a wide range of good principles that we are already formulating today in our policy on disability: the principle of equal treatment, because everyone must have equal opportunities to realise their dreams and potentials; the principle of compensation, because we must help children, young people and adults to overcome the barriers that prevent them from participating in society on an equal footing with everyone else; the principle of sectoral responsibility, because we must jointly lift and ensure that all offers across sectors are available to people with disabilities; and not least the principle of solidarity, because help and support should not depend on the individual's income. At the same time, we constantly assess whether all new legislation is in line with Denmark's international obligations, including the obligations of the Disability Convention. In their comments on the motion for a resolution, the authors refer to the 2014 report of the Committee on Human Rights. Here the committee pointed out that there could be a risk of transferring powers from Parliament and government to the courts and other public authorities by incorporating international conventions. The risk lies in the fact that, among other things, The disability convention contains a number of vague and general provisions which are not suitable to be applied directly in the handling of cases before the courts and the authorities. At the same time, incorporation creates a stronger basis for interpreting the Disability Convention in the light of the practice of the UN Disability Committee. Despite this, the Committee recommended the incorporation of, inter alia: The disability convention. However, the Committee noted that the Disability Convention was the most recent international convention ratified by Denmark and that the practice of the UN Disability Committee was sparing. The Committee is set up by the Convention and its 18 members from all over the world have the task of interpreting the application of the Convention in the Member States; members who do not necessarily have a greater insight into the construction of our political system and Danish welfare societies. Back in 2014, the Committee had only adopted two general observations on the understanding of Article 12 of the Convention on Equality before the Law and Article 9 on Availability. The number is now seven in total, and the number of statements in complaints for citizens has also increased from 6 in 2014 to 18 here in 2021. What does the committee's practice show us? Practice shows, among other things, that the risk of a shift of power from the Folketing to the courts is real. A good example of this is a case against Denmark from 2018. The Committee found that the Danish authorities' refusal to reunite families represented indirect discrimination against people with disabilities. The Supreme Court had given a ruling in the case before which it was found that, by refusing family reunification, complaints had not been discriminated against in breach of the European Convention on Human Rights or the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities. The Government thus disagreed with the Committee's application of the Convention. The policy adopted jointly by the Folketing and the government is a national matter. The inclusion of the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities must not be allowed to upset that. Denmark must be vigilant about international conventions and we must respect them at all times. We must continue to make the principles and rights of the disability convention one of the cornerstones of Danish disability policy. We have to do that right until the goal is achieved and we all have it. As part of this work, the government launched last year together with the Radical Left, SF, Enhedslisten and the Alternative evaluation of the specialized social area. The evaluation must provide us with the in-depth knowledge needed to better shape the area and ensure that there are the right offers and efforts of the right specialisation and quality, which correspond to the needs of citizens, and which help young people and children and adults with disabilities to have the best opportunities to live an independent and meaningful life on their own terms. The incorporation of international conventions is not something we are committed to or have a tradition of doing. As a citizen, conventions can already be invoked directly in the courts or other authorities. An incorporation won't change that. At the same time, we must ensure that Danish policy remains a policy adopted in this Chamber alone. The government therefore supports the intentions behind the proposal, but we cannot support the proposal itself. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517105522	Thank you. Thank you to the Radical Left for focusing on people with disabilities and their rights. The main reason for this resolution is that it will ensure a greater obligation to clarify the possibility for citizens to invoke the conventions in the courts. In addition, the resolution also points out that full incorporation will send a positive signal that Denmark will strengthen the rights of people with disabilities on an equal footing with other Western countries. Finally, the motion for a resolution points out that incorporation will have a great symbolic significance at national and international level, because people with disabilities and their rights are unfortunately still being suppressed. The Left agrees that it is necessary to strengthen the rights of people with disabilities. We are because the Left has a desire that as many people as possible with disabilities should become an active part of the community and that they should have equal opportunities to take an education or to maintain links with the labour market. The Left takes people with disabilities and their rights very seriously, because it is of no use to talk about equal treatment for all people if we cannot also ensure that we protect people with disabilities in accordance with the law and the provisions of the Convention on Disability. With that said, however, the Left is sceptical about the incorporation of the Disability Convention. We are concerned that full incorporation into Danish law will lead to more disadvantages than there will be benefits. We are already under an obligation to protect people with disabilities and their rights in accordance with the provisions of the Disability Convention. The incorporation of the Disability Convention will not change that. Furthermore, it is already possible to invoke the Disability Convention before the courts and other judicial authorities, and the incorporation of the Disability Convention will not change that either. In the Left, we are concerned that full incorporation into the Convention in Danish law will bring the disadvantages and risks which, among other things, will arise. The Committee on Human Rights in 2014 highlighted with their report on inclusion in the area of human rights, and as the Minister has also mentioned in his speech. The Committee points out that the UN Convention on Disabilities contains a number of provisions which are vague and generally formulated, making the provisions less suitable to be incorporated, and this would mean that there is a risk that incorporation into the Convention on Disabilities into Danish law will lead to a shift of power from the Folketing to the courts in relation to issues which, according to Danish legal tradition, are the responsibility of the legislature. This may be quite problematic, because there will then be a risk that some of the powers of the Danish Parliament will be transferred to the Danish courts and to other judicial authorities, where we do not have the democratic debate that we have in here and which do not necessarily have the full overview of general political and economic considerations. Against this background, the Left cannot support the motion for a resolution. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517111100	Thank you. In Denmark we help each other and we give each other a chance and the opportunity to live the life we want, regardless of who we are and where we come from. For although Denmark ratified the UN Disability Convention back in 2009, it is clear to most that work is far from over. We believe that people with disabilities must be able to live a decent and active life on their own terms and on their own terms. There are many specific places we can put right. We can, for example. talk about the BPA. Personalised assistance is a fantastic solution which allows citizens with greater disability to organise their own assistance so that they can live the self-employed and active life they want. Unfortunately, the scheme has been eroded and undermined, and we must have done something about this, because the BPA is precisely one of the tools that allows people with disabilities to do something to adapt their help so that they can live the life they want, and not just what is cheapest for the municipality, which is a problem in this area. We must also ensure that, as a citizen with disabilities, you are not tied to your home or to a particular municipality in fear of losing the help that has been given. One must also be sure that the municipality does not mistreat cases and the process is dragged out to save money, or because the file is too large in the Ankestyrelsen. You should not just lose the help you receive or be fundamentally disadvantaged just because you are 18 years old and therefore go from child to adult. Instead, we need to focus on how we can help the citizen move forward with a full life that everyone else has, with education and jobs, possibly their own housing or community of living and leisure, and we need to ensure that the aids are given to those who need it to live a full, active and reasonably normal life. There are enough problems to address in the area of disability, but more fundamental changes are also needed. We must fundamentally change the way we see people with disabilities. We must give them support and the help they need to be able to live as normal and actively as possible. We should not just park them on a performance in a bad environment where they have no freedom. We must ensure that people with disabilities in Denmark have the same basic opportunities and rights as everyone else, and basically it is also the expectation that we all have of our welfare society, that is to say, whoever you are, you also have the opportunity to live a good life. That is exactly what the UN Convention on Disability provides. There are no unreasonable demands, but merely demands for fair adjustment, that is. We must be able to deliver on this in a strong welfare society. Therefore, if we all believe that everyone in Denmark should have equal opportunities and rights, we should take the next step in incorporating the disability convention into our national legislation. We are ready for this in the SF, which is why we naturally support this motion for a resolution.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517111520	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no questions. And the next rapporteur is Mrs Jette Gottlieb, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517111542	Thank you. I have to inform you that our rapporteur in this area, Mr President. Jakob Silverhøj cannot be present, and that is why I put forward his views. It is now 12 years since the Danish Parliament decided to ratify the United Nations Disability Convention, and Enhedslisten agrees with the proposers that it is time to incorporate the Disability Convention into Danish law. The motion for a resolution refers to the report produced by the Committee on Human Rights back in 2014. As the comments on the proposal show, there was no agreement in the Committee on Human Rights on the recommendations concerning the incorporation of the seven conventions examined by the Committee. Of the 15 members of the Committee, the five were appointed by the then government and they failed to make recommendations, while among the remaining ten there was a small majority recommending the incorporation of most of the conventions, including the Disability Convention. This majority pointed out that incorporation would strengthen citizens' legal protection and send out an unequivocal signal that we in Denmark want to ensure respect for people with disabilities' rights. However, it is the opinion of the six advocates that there is no real risk of such a shift of power. The reality is, of course, that the actual form of incorporation is precisely designed in the Folketing, with all that that implies of democratic and political debate. Therefore, based on an overall assessment of the advantages and disadvantages, it is the recommendation of the six members of the Committee to incorporate the Convention in order to strengthen citizens with disabilities' legal protection. It was regrettable that the report back in 2014 did not lead to the incorporation of the Convention. It is a pity that we have left countries such as France and Germany in this area as pioneering countries, and we do not think that we should wait any longer. That is why the Enhedslisten supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517111817	The discussion which the Radical Left is raising here today on the incorporation of the UN Convention on Disability is important. As the authors also mention in the comments, Denmark has fortunately ratified the Convention, and so we have an obligation to organise national legislation so that it is working towards the objective within the framework of the Disability Convention. I do not believe that there are two opinions in this Chamber today that citizens with disabilities deserve a life in which, of course, they have the best conditions to live as normal as possible. Because in a welfare society like the Danish one, we must be able to expect more and provide the best framework for a good life. ParlaMint-DK_20210517112247	We thank the rapporteur. There are no questions. And that is why it is now the rapporteur for the authors of the motion, Mr President. Kristian Hegaard, Radical. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517112342	Thank you for the floor. Right to education, the right to participate in political and public life, the right to accessibility, the right to protection of personal integrity, the right to dignity, yes, just some of the few very fundamental and basic rights enshrined in the UN Convention on Disability. Denmark has ratified it in 2006 and entered into force in 2009. In 2017, we even received in Denmark a law prohibiting discrimination against people with disabilities outside the labour market, and it came into force in 2018. Therefore, one should immediately think that it is going the right way in relation to the rights of people with disabilities in Denmark. Unfortunately, we all know in here about the towering rates of change in the Ankestyrelsen and, as Danske Handicaporganisationer writes on their website today prior to this motion for a resolution, a few thousand citizens are still away who are not guaranteed the right to vote in the elections in the Folketing because they have a disability. The Institute of Human Rights has revealed that five out of six Danish transport companies will not help people with physical disabilities on board regular buses. One third of all victims of violence and sexual abuse have a cognitive or mental disability according to the Institute for Human Rights. And according to VIVE is only 58 percent. of all people with disabilities in employment as against 84 per cent. of all people without disabilities. Let us therefore take the step fully with the UN Disability Convention and incorporate rights for people with disabilities into Danish legislation. For example, looking at the UN recommendations on human rights to Denmark, in 2016 there were eight proposals for improvements related to people with disabilities, and in 2021 there were 15 proposals for improvements for people with disabilities. So there's room for improvement, I guess. One of the reasons why the Radical Left has raised this motion for a resolution today is that our minister, in consultation on 11 February 2021 1 hour, 50 minutes and 11 seconds inside the television broadcast, said: "I will assume at all times that we are living up to the disability convention and that we are living up to that responsibility'. A natural part of that would be to incorporate the Disability Convention into our legislation, for as the Committee on Inclusion, etc. in the field of human rights, in its report in 2014: "Incorporation will, in the Committee's view, lead in itself to clarification of the possibilities for private individuals to invoke the Convention before national courts'. The Committee stresses in particular that it is important for the intensity of the review; that is what, in legal language, means the depth of the assessment of compliance. I suppose it is a good thing to increase the opportunity to invoke the rights that I assume we agree that as a person with disabilities you have. In France, the disability convention is directly applicable, in Germany it has been incorporated as part of German law by a convention law. Would it not be good if Denmark were a country which was at least on the same footing as the countries with which we compare ourselves when it comes to guaranteeing people with disability rights? And that, as the Committee on Inclusion also writes, will give a positive signal in the international context, and when we have to go out into the world and convince others that they need to improve people with disabilities' rights, it will be strong that we ourselves have incorporated them into our own legislation. With regard to the majority of the committee, I would just like to point out that there were 6 in committee who spoke in favour and 4 against; so there were five who abstained because they were representatives of the ministries and therefore did not think they would give their opinion. So when there are 6 out of 15, it may sound as if it were not a majority, but in practice it was a majority because it was 6 to 4. And with the 6 against the 4 the committee gave the opinion that the convention is particularly suitable for incorporation into Danish legislation, because it is a group of people who are at particular risk of intervention and abuse. That is why a majority of the committee considered that the Convention should be incorporated into Danish legislation. At the same time, I would like to highlight a decision by the Central Jutland State Administration in 2012, which states that the Convention does not create an immediate right for citizens to invoke the conventions before the authorities. It was in relation to a case where a citizen tried it. And that is something we can change, so that the authorities cannot fail to have an accurate review of the UN Convention on Disability in their decision. That is why I would like to thank the parties, i.e. the SF and the Enhedslisten, whom I hear supported the motion for a resolution on incorporation into Danish law. I'm really glad. I have not heard in the debate anyone who was outright opposed to any of the articles of the UN Convention on Disability. That is why one might well wonder why, if one is not against the content, one does not think that we should incorporate it into Danish legislation. The main counter-argument has been that the majority is afraid, as it were, to shift power to the courts. It is therefore feared that rights will be established here which the courts will have to assess whether they have been respected. But that's what they do every day. It is therefore feared that the right to education and training will be respected in the courts; whether the right to participate in political and public life has been respected; whether the right to accessibility has been respected; whether the right to protection of personal integrity has been respected; whether the right to dignity has been respected, etc. etc. If you are afraid that the courts in Denmark will have to make that assessment, then first I am seriously concerned. That is why we must, of course, incorporate the disability convention into Danish law rather today than tomorrow.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517113730	Thank you for that, President. We are now discussing motion for a resolution B 218. That has been put forward by the Left. With the proposal, the proposers will require the government to launch by 1 June this year a comprehensive pilot project with older counsellors to inform about the various private providers of services. The aim of the proposal is to guide older citizens and possibly relatives to make a free choice of service provider. The proposal provides for the development of a final model for older counsellors in relation to suppliers' choices that can be rolled out across the country. In the government, we want a welfare society based on the needs of the individual elderly and where each elderly person experiences self-determination and influence on his or her own life. For me and for the government, what is important is not whether it is a private or public supplier that vacuums in the citizen's home. What is important, however, is that care is organized according to the needs of the individual citizen. Unlike the proposers, we in the government have a broader approach to free choice with a greater focus on self-determination and the needs of the individual elderly than just a focus on the free choice of service provider. A dignified care for the elderly, where we can all be confident that our loved ones will have the help and care they need when they themselves can no longer, if old age is pressing, is about keeping their hands under welfare financially, that the funds will come with when more older people arrive. This government has secured this in the economic agreements we have made with the municipalities. It is also about ensuring that we have an elderly care where there is time and space to reach and quality between the employees and the elderly and their relatives, where it is not all eaten up by bureaucracy, but where the self-determination and quality of life of the elderly are at the centre every day. In order to ensure a dignified care for the elderly, with the greatest possible self-determination for older citizens, the government has launched a number of initiatives which focus on finding new ways and ways of strengthening the care of the elderly to the benefit of the elderly and the elderly's relatives and to the fight against bureaucracy and minute tyranny. One of the initiatives of our very first financial agreement and thanks to the Finance Law parties for it was a pool to strengthen care and presence in the care of the elderly. In this respect, 245 million were set aside. kr., which is now translated into 31 different projects that develop and test new ways to strengthen the care and presence of citizens in our care for the elderly. A concrete example here is about testing the core elements of the Buurtzorg model, getting it evaluated and developed in a Danish context. It is also the cooperation with citizens and relatives and autonomous teams, which are interdisciplinary, which must help ensure coherence for the citizen in a flexible allocation of aid, where the citizen and the employee constantly adapt the help that the citizen needs, because two citizens are not the same, and two days are not the same in the life of an elderly citizen. In addition to that, there are projects in the pool that look at ways to reduce unnecessary documentation requirements so that we can ensure that time is spent on the individual elderly citizen. There is a project to reverse the search process and reduce the requirement for documentation to free up time and profit to care and presence. And there are many other very, very exciting development projects that I follow closely. Another initiative is the so-called Freedom Communities, in other words the welfare agreements concluded between the government and three specific municipalities in the area of the elderly, where we in the Danish Parliament, with legislation, have also freed the municipalities from most of the state rules, and local authorities have done the same for the municipal rules. We have turned the bucket upside down and made the most radical freeing attempt ever. The aim is clear: that with freedom comes responsibility; older citizens must experience greater presence and a greater space for the care and care and needs of the individual elderly in everyday life. Finally, the government and the parties from the senior summit have started work on precisely the organisation of visitation and governance. The aim is to strengthen the work undertaken to develop new forms of organisation of care for the elderly under different auspices, with more or less autonomous teams around citizens. The aim is freed management, greater professional space, close dialogue between citizens, relatives and employees on how the aid is organised and organised in everyday life. So we in the government have a strong focus on self-determination and also a focus on self-determination and the needs of the individual, which go beyond the free choice of service provider. With the proposal we are discussing here today, more obligations and more bureaucracy apply to the municipalities, and that is despite the fact that there are already rules in place for municipal councils to provide information on the possibilities for free choice of service providers. Here, then, again, is a proposal from Christiansborg, where the funds go not to care and presence, but to administration. It is therefore without reason that, as part of its general obligation to provide information on the possibilities of freely choosing the provider of services, the municipal council must inform the recipients of home help of which authorized providers of the assistance the citizen can choose from. More unnecessary bureaucracy, more unnecessary obligations are not the way to decent care for the elderly, in which we create a better framework for professionalism, care and presence. With more bureaucracy, however, we are stifling the opportunities for the individual citizen to experience more presence, better care and better care. To spend 50 million. kr. in a pilot project on older counsellors in relation to suppliers' choice, which will result in more bureaucracy, more rules and more obligations in an area where legislation is already in place, is not, in my view, a sensible use of so much money. In addition, the proposers refer to the financial priorities of the Left for 2021, where among other things: The proposal here is being financed through a roll-back of the agreement on the new entitlement to early retirement. Nor can the government support that funding. As I said, I share the aims of the proposers and the desire for dignified care for the elderly, with the greatest possible self-determination for the citizens, but more obligations and more bureaucracy for the municipalities are not the right way to go. It hits the employees, and it hits the elderly. That is why the government cannot support the proposal. As I have said, it is partly because of the bureaucratic consequences in an area where there are already obligations in the legislation that pursue the same purpose, but also because the government looks much wider on the care of the elderly than just the free choice of service providers. We are working instead of finding new ways and new ways of ensuring self-determination and quality of life for older citizens and dignified care for the elderly, which is not organised on the basis of schedules and minute tyranny, but on the basis of the needs of the specific elderly.

In this context, it should be pointed out that there is a need for the local authorities to carry out an attempt to do something to which the municipalities are already obliged. The municipalities already do that today, for example. on the municipality's website, orally or by handing over a leaflet. It is, in fact, a requirement that already exists today. In the Social Democratic Party we do not want to create more obligations and more bureaucracy in the municipalities by making additional rules or arrangements on information and guidance, and although this is only an attempt on this proposal, we do not exactly expect the experiment to lead to useful experiences that we can necessarily continue with. We are aiming for less bureaucracy for the municipalities, and instead we would very much like to create the framework for more professionalism, more care and more presence in the care of the elderly, and to allocate 50 million. kr. to a pilot project aimed at fulfilling existing commitments, we do not think that is the right way to go to achieve that objective. Of course, in the social democracy we want more dignity in the care of the elderly, and we want it to be possible for employees, together with the individual citizen, to be able to organise care and help in the light of the needs of the individual, and it is not at all crucial whether it is public or private suppliers who provide care, but whether the care is organized according to the needs of the citizen. I just want to say that right now it is sizzling and bubbling out in the municipalities to stifle unnecessary governance and bureaucracy, and everyone wants to get rid of this so that there will be more time for presence. Already with the 2020 Finance Act, 245 million was allocated. kr. for various projects designed to develop and test new innovative ways of strengthening the care and presence of citizens in the care of the elderly, looking at ways of reducing unnecessary documentation requirements, so that more time is spent on the individual citizen and of course also on talking to the individual citizen about which offers suit the citizen best. The government has also started work on the welfare agreement, as the Minister just mentioned, so I will not spend so much of my speaking time on that. Finally, the government and the parties in the context of the Elder Summit have also launched a work on organisation and searching and management, the aim of which is to strengthen the work being done to develop new forms of organisation of care for the elderly with smaller teams around citizens. The aim here is to free management, provide greater professional space and allow for close dialogue between citizens and relatives and employees on how to organise and run the aid on a daily basis. Finally, we should also point out that the Municipality Agreement for 2021 actually states that the government and the KL agree that the current governance and regulation in several welfare areas has become too complex and has too much focus on how we deliver welfare rather than on what welfare we provide. So, on the left, we will say no to spending 50 million. kr. on a task already to be undertaken by the municipalities. Citizens today have the opportunity to choose freely and to obtain the necessary information from the municipalities about what choices they can make. For us, it is important that we spend the money on more welfare rather than on who provides the welfare. So we cannot vote in favour of the proposal. I should say hello from the Enhedslisten and say that neither can they. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517133938	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister for Social Affairs and the elderly. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517134006	The motion for a resolution here proposes requiring the government to set up a working group to investigate the possibility of temporarily excluding or denying a person the right to work in the social field if the person is under reasonable suspicion or accused of having committed an act of violence or other gross negligence in the course of the work. The reason for the proposal is a specific case of an employee in a settlement who was accused of violence against a resident and who subsequently continued to work in the social field without the new workplace being familiar with the person's history. He was subsequently sentenced to 40 days in jail and also deprived of the right to work in care for 3 years. I fully share the intention of the authors of the motion for a resolution that, as a resident or user of a social offer, we should be able to feel safe and secure and that we should not in any way accept violence, threatening or degrading behaviour against our vulnerable citizens, certainly not from the staff entrusted with the task of helping and caring for them. Although I share the concerns and intentions of the proposers, the government cannot support the resolution because we believe that we already have different safeguards where the same objective is achieved in different ways. A case such as the one mentioned must not be repeated, but if it does happen, one of the first things that can and should be done as a management is, of course, to disfellowship the employee from the workplace, so that the employment relationship ends immediately and so that he no longer has contact with the citizen. This is an effective approach, and it was also done in the case in question. In addition, we have various safeguards to prevent the wrong people from being employed on social services. First of all, since 1 July 2020, it has been a legal requirement that the management of each offer obtains a criminal record prior to the appointment of staff in social services covered by the Social Protection Agency. The requirement includes both employees, temporary workers and managers. In addition, management has the opportunity to obtain recommendations and opinions from the person's previous workplace. Both obtaining a criminal record and obtaining recommendations from previous employers require the consent of the job seeker. If a job seeker does not want to give up a reference, it can in itself make the alarm bells ring. The possibility of obtaining references from former employers is, I believe, a long way from being able to act as a safeguard against hiring the wrong people, especially when they have not been convicted of a criminal offence, which would not be seen by the criminal record. It is also apparent from the websites of several municipalities that they obtain references with prior informed consent. I believe that the management has a clear interest in examining the history of the job seeker in question, so that they have the best possible staff to carry out the task in the offer and to prevent incidents of violence or equivalent. I also believe that there can be no doubt in the social sphere that a great deal of responsibility has been placed on individual management in the offers of hiring staff. I expect you as a leader to take on that responsibility so that you get the right employees in. To go back to where I started, I am pleased that the proposers share the concern for our vulnerable children, young people and adults and people with disabilities, and that we all want them to have a safe everyday life with the help and support they need. This is an agenda which the government is very concerned about. However, I do not believe that we will solve the problem, as the specific case showed, by setting up a working group to examine the possibility of temporarily excluding or denying a person the right to take up work in the social field. In the social field, we are already ahead of having demands to help the wrong people not be employed in social services, among other things. in the form of the requirement to obtain criminal records. At the same time, there is also the possibility of examining the history of job applicants, which I would strongly urge the individual local management to make use of. Then, of course, you are innocent until proven otherwise, and that is why we must generally be cautious about depriving someone of the right to work while being accused of criminal law. In that case, a management will be able to expel the person from the place, as happened in the particular case. Against this background, the government cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517142237	Thank you for that, President. In the Danish People's Party, we want a working group to be set up to investigate the possibility of temporarily excluding or denying a person the right to work in the social field. The proposal has been made on the basis of a creepy case from 2020 when an employee at a North Jutland settlement was charged with beating a heavily devel oped resident. The employee was employed as a support teacher on a special educational residence offer for adults, and the employee was charged with beating the resident while the resident was hospitalized. The whole incident was videotaped and there was therefore no particular uncertainty associated with the charge. Nevertheless, the defendant has been free to continue working in the social field, just somewhere else. The new employer has no opportunity to know about the employee's history until the employee has received a verdict, and even then it is not certain that the employer will ever learn that the employee has a verdict and for what he has been convicted. In the Danish People's Party, we do not think it is right that an employee who has committed violence against one of society's most vulnerable and vulnerable citizens should be able to proceed to a new workplace and a new employer who does not know about the employee's past. In the field of health, health professionals may temporarily be deprived of their authorisation if they are considered to be a risk to patient safety, even if no judgment has yet been handed down. Something similar should be the case in the social field, so that we do not run the risk that an employee who is clearly a danger to the citizens with whom he or she works can simply continue his or her working life in the social field elsewhere in the country. Paragraphs 78 and 79 of the Criminal Code provide for a certain degree of freedom to exclude an employee from engaging in certain activities if they have received a judgment in respect of a situation which makes there an obvious danger of abuse of the post or profession. There is section 79 of the Criminal Code. 4, also the possibility of being excluded from the exercise of activities in this field until the case is finally settled. The problem with paragraphs 78 and 79 is that they require a judgment to be delivered and the problem with paragraphs 78 and 79 is that they require a judgment to be delivered. 4 of Paragraph 79 states that the provision must be applied restrictively so that it is very difficult to use it in practice. In the specific case of the employee who struck the resident, the prosecution considered that the provision of Paragraph 79 (1) of the Civil Code was not applicable. 4, could not be used even if the violence was recorded on video, and it is almost difficult to imagine a case where there was better evidence that an employee had committed violence against a resident. So, if the provision of Paragraph 79 of the Criminal Code... 4, cannot be used in such a case, we as a society need to look at how we can ensure that employees can be temporarily excluded from working in the social field if there is a reasonable suspicion that the individual employee has committed violence or gross negligence towards a physically or mentally exposed citizen. It may also take a long time for a case to be settled in court, and it is not fair to expose our socially vulnerable citizens to the risk that an employee accused of violence against another resident can just get work elsewhere in the social area. That is why we in the Danish People's Party propose that the Minister, with the assistance of other ministries, set up a working group to examine the possibilities and recommendations for arrangements to temporarily exclude or deny a person the right to be employed in other positions in the social field if there is a reasonable suspicion that he or she has committed violence or gross negligence towards a physically or mentally socially exposed citizen. And, of course, the intention is that this should apply to employees in the social field who are in direct contact with socially vulnerable citizens with physical and psychological impairments, including home care, in institutions, in communities of residence and similar places. We in the Danish People's Party have described some solutions that the working group can include in its study, but we think it is important to find the best possible solution, so that the solutions we have described should not in any way bind the working group or take precedence over other good solutions. And we then believe that the outcome of the working group should be presented to the parties of the Folketing by 1 December 2021 and, precisely on this basis, form the basis for some negotiations among the rapporteurs. However, I am sorry to say that there will not be any support for this, and I am very incomprehensible about this, precisely in the light of all the examples we have seen. I think it is very good that the majority of our staff working in the care and care sector are skilled and respectful employees who have empathy and heart in the right place, and these are not the ones that this proposal is about. But every time we have to have a debate in this Parliament, which is about the broken vessels, it is always, I think, a little difficult for many of the other social rapporteurs. Because it is precisely the stingy vessels it is about, and New Citizens said it very well from the platform, and we all remember the ugly, ugly episode where the demented woman was subjected to a very, very violent and ugly way. We need to think about what kind of staff we want to take care of for some of the most vulnerable and vulnerable citizens, and now several rapporteurs here today have said that the system we have is good enough. However, I do not feel at all that it is good enough when we can see that our vulnerable citizens are still failing, that stray vessels continue their work. In the Danish People's Party, we have fought to make it compulsory for both private and public suppliers to obtain public criminal records. This has been achieved in some of the social areas, in other words, precisely where the social inspectorate is supervising. However, it is the case that you can then be employed by a private supplier, and private suppliers are not obliged to obtain public criminal records. Nor is there any provision in the municipalities for obtaining public criminal records, but that is something they can do. So there is a need to prioritise this area and ensure that the vulnerable and vulnerable citizens who need help are not let down, and if we can do anything on the part of the Folketing in any way whatsoever, then I think we should solve it. We have put forward many proposals and one more in order to find a solution in this area, because we think it is so important, precisely because we can see that massive failures are taking place today. ParlaMint-DK_20210517143038	The debate is open. The first to speak is the Minister for Health and the elderly. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517143101	The motion for a resolution here sets out some principles for the estimation of cases of association which the proposers believe will lead to better co-decision and bring Danish law into line with the conventions. The aim of the proposal is to put an end to the harassment of cooperation, which means that some parents do not see their children. Let me state straight away that I fully agree with the underlying intention. It is quite unacceptable if a parent for no reason prevents the child from seeing the other parent. Unless there is a really good reason why the other parent should not be a part of the child's life. be because the parent is convicted of perilous crime... then it is clearly best for children to have good and close contact with both their parents. Having said that, the government cannot support the proposal. We cannot do this because the proposal focuses on the right of parents rather than on what is most important, namely the child. The comments on the proposal begin with the view that the right of parents to associate with their children must be regarded as one of the most fundamental human rights. I agree, of course, that the most important thing for each parent is to be with his or her child and that the links between children and parents are protected by the Convention on Human Rights, but there are therefore cases where consideration for the child must be given to the highest weight and where it may be necessary to interrupt the child's contact with one parent. One can hardly disagree with the proposers that the family and our children of the most precious thing we have. Too far, the vast majority of children, it is better for them to keep in touch with both parents when the family is broken up; there is no doubt about that. I note that the proposers are aware of the fact that the parental responsibility law states that the child's best should come first, but then it is also as if the proposal is based on a wrong premise. It should be noted, in fact, that the law does not specify which other considerations are factual and relevant when deciding on association and that the law and the administration of the proceedings are contrary to the conventions because other relevant and objective considerations are not taken into account. That's just not right. First of all, the parents' liability laws make it clear that the assessment of what is best for the child must always be based on an individual assessment of the individual child's circumstances. In fact, it is stated immediately that consideration must be given to whether one parent, without reasonable reason, hinders the association of the other parent. Secondly, the parental responsibility law directly states that contact between children and parents must be maintained when parents break up. This is, in fact, the very clear starting point of the law, and it is also the starting point from which the Family House and family law dealt with matters. This means that in the vast majority of cases dealt with by the authorities there is also provision for child-parental relations. But here we come to something very central that is very important to stick to, namely that it is about the child's When the new divorce system was agreed in 2019, all parties agreed that consideration for the child is paramount and must always come before all other considerations. It is now established in the law with seven-inch nails, and that is the consideration we must hold on to. One must be made of stone to avoid feeling for the children who are in trouble in the most conflicting divorce cases there, where parents have often fought for the child for years. There are cases where the conflict arises because of risk factors such as: abuse and violence, but there are also cases where it is difficult to see the cause of the conflict. The fact is that whatever lies behind the conflicts, in some cases they are so violent that children pay too high a price. Years ago, Children's Conditions interviewed a group of children who had all been through some very complex and conflicting divorces. In some cases, as I mentioned earlier, there were parents with abuse and mental illness, but there were also cases without such risk factors. One of the children told in the Children's Terms study that at the age of 5 she could say the State Administration and that the parents were through 13 different court cases • 13 court cases! These are precisely the children that the law and the system must protect and on which the new divorce system has the greatest focus. For me, it is not primarily a question of being suitable as parents or not, but, on the contrary, first of all, of what is best for the child, because unfortunately, the fact is that these children can be so insecure in the very heavy and worst conflicts that, in the worst cases, it can be best for the child, where the conflicts are greatest, that there is no association, even if both parents on paper are suitable to care for the child. Moreover, it is in full agreement with the conventions that we have signed and, when it is stipulated in the law that the child's relationship with parents must be maintained when the family breaks up, it is in accordance with the right to family life under the European Convention on Human Rights. At the same time, however, and in full compliance with the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child, the law also states that the child's best should be given priority in all decisions concerning the child. It is a consideration that must be overwhelmed by all considerations. The authors themselves point out that this is reflected in practice by the European Court of Human Rights. Unfortunately, we have to deal with the fact that the reality is that there are parents harassing each other. It is the parents who create the conflicts, not the law or the authorities. That said, both the law and the authorities must tackle the harassment so that the child's relationship with both parents is maintained. Over time, therefore, there has also been a great deal of focus on cooperation harassment, and it has put its mark both in the law and in the way the system can intervene. For example, a legal amendment from 2015 with the so-called harassment package has. In the case of an association which is cancelled for no reason, an automatic right to compensation has been introduced in the House of Families. Cooperation harassment can also be a determining factor in which parents should have custody of the child, as expressed in the Supreme Court ruling referred to in the proposal. After years of harassment, a father was given custody because the court considered that he is the one of the parents who will clearly best be able to make a future relationship work so that the child can have good contact with both his parents. In conclusion, I do not see why the principles proposed would give better decisions or eliminate harassment. On the contrary, the proposal focuses too clearly on the right of parents, so that in some cases it becomes more important than the right of the child. In my view, this is the wrong way to go. If anyone asks me whose side I'm on, it will always be the child's side. We must therefore insist that consideration for the child is essential, as was also stated in the reform of the family law area, otherwise we will be abandoning children who are already vulnerable and squeezed by years of conflict between parents. The government cannot support this, which is why we cannot support the proposal here.

The practice of fellowship chicane is very problematic and attempted solved in a relatively new law. The punishment of fellowship chaikas is shown by a Supreme Court ruling. In the SF we would like to support further investigations into the area, but we cannot support the concrete proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517151917	The debate is open. First of all, we welcome the Minister for Social Affairs and the Minister for the Elderly.
ParlaMint-DK_20210517151945	Thank you, President. The motion for a resolution B 156, which we are about to discuss here, proposes that before 1 May 2021 the government should put forward a legislative proposal setting up a new pilot scheme with extended free municipalities. The extended free municipality experiment scheme should enable all 98 municipalities to apply for specific experimental courses in one or more welfare areas of their choice. It could, for example, be done. be day-to-day, primary school, older or employment areas. With the extended free-community scheme, all the municipalities in the country should have much greater freedom to find new and effective solutions themselves. Over the years, a number of good efforts have been made to eliminate unnecessary bureaucracy in the municipal sector. We have had free municipalities in various forms, and the right to challenge was introduced in 2008 and we have removed individual provisions, but this must be said to have been a sporadic effort with various tools. Despite good intentions, there remains a need to debureaucratise and simplify rules. The former government of VLAK launched in 2019 Free Municipality Experiment III. 20 applications were received from a total of 17 municipalities, so there was a limited interest from the municipalities in a new free municipality experiment. The applications were subsequently redirected to the scope of the extended challenge rights in December 2019. The previous government also launched the second round of free institution attempts in 2019 where both municipalities and institutions could apply for experiments. However, in March 2020 the government decided not to carry out the trials. This was partly because there was very limited interest in the experiments among the municipalities. Only 3 out of 98 municipalities had shown interest in this despite the fact that the experiment had been open to all the municipalities in the country. The government's attitude is also based on the fact that we have tried and tested the experimental schemes where individual rules are removed. This does not mean, of course, that we simply have to give up more attempts, where ideas to better solve welfare tasks, with less bureaucratic rules, can come from below, from those who know where the rules are bothering and preventing new and better ways of doing things, on the contrary. But there is a need for us to try something new, and that is precisely what the government has done with the welfare agreements in the day-to-day services sector, the school sector and in the care of the elderly. The proposers state that the extended free municipality attempt must free all 98 municipalities at once and that the municipalities themselves must be able to choose one or more welfare areas where the municipality must be freed. On that suggestion, I may have a quiet wonder. In the past, the proposers have refused to support the welfare agreements in the area of the elderly. The proposers have quite recently voted against draft legislation on welfare agreements in the area of the elderly. Now you want it all and more. How it works, I don't know. With the welfare agreements, we are going a different and new way than we have done before. We start, so to speak, with a blank piece of paper, because we will do away with what chokes professionalism and presence. We don't know best in here at Christiansborg... not always at least. This makes those who are daily close to the older citizen, the child in kindergarten or the pupil in primary school. Citizens must experience more presence, better care, better care. They can do so by allowing the professionally competent staff to have a freer framework to create better welfare with the citizens. It takes courage, and it requires faith in the professional: the teachers, the educators and the employees of the elderly centres, and the courage and dedication of the local authorities have been shown by the seven municipalities. I'd like to sign for that. Experiments of this nature can only work if there is strong political anchoring in the municipalities. Of course, children still need high quality education. The elderly still need the help and care they need. Therefore, on the blank piece of paper, a few fence posts have been set up to ensure that this happens. But with the greater freedom granted to the municipalities, there is, of course, also a responsibility to deliver better welfare and better solutions. I believe that the seven municipalities can deliver that. The municipalities have so much spirit and lots of ideas. The welfare agreements are an experiment in depth in which the municipalities are given far-reaching degrees of freedom. The number of municipalities involved is therefore also a balance in relation to the extent of degrees of freedom. As I have already mentioned, it is therefore most appropriate at this stage to start with seven municipalities, each of which is completely free from unnecessary regulation in a single area, where we try a wild idea on a smaller scale. We will have much better conditions if we have the opportunity to follow and support each of the seven municipalities. It therefore requires close cooperation, and it requires some municipalities dedicated to the experiment. The same is true in relation to the number of welfare areas. We start with three welfare areas that form the cornerstone of close welfare: day care, primary school and care for the elderly. Unnecessary bureaucracy, detailed regulation is certainly also needed in other welfare areas, so experience should not only benefit in three welfare areas and in seven municipalities. It is clearly our ambition to be able to spread the experience of attempts to improve the welfare of the rest of the country's municipalities. This does not mean, of course, that other measures or proposals for freedom can be implemented in parallel with the welfare agreements in the broad sense. The important thing here is to look at the purpose and why we are doing these attempts: to remove bureaucracy, to make room for the professionalism of employees and local solutions close to the citizens. So to sum up: instead of the various liberation attempts that we have had over the past many years, for which there has not been a great deal of interest, we need to let go and do things in a completely new way. With the political agreements on the welfare agreements, we have undertaken to turn the whole thing upside down. We're gonna have to step back in here. We need to see innovation sprude, and that is why it is a question of dare to go quite far. I can assure the Left and the rest of the parliamentary parties that the government's work to debureaucratise the public sector does not end with the welfare agreements. On the contrary, it is only a first step. So here I think the Left kicks in an open door, so to speak. For, of course, we must continue the work of releasing our citizens and skilled employees from tighter retail management. Welfare agreements on day-to-day services, public schools and the elderly are the starting point: several areas can be reached. But we don't do it all at once. We have tried this before with limited results and also with limited interest on the part of the municipalities, I dare say. In other words, we are doing this step by step in order to be able to support the experiments. That option will not be there if all municipalities and welfare areas are released at the same time. As the experience of the previous free municipalities also shows, it is about quality and not quantity in the experiments and in the number of participating municipalities, if we are to succeed in putting something seriously on this agenda. I look forward very much to getting started in earnest and seeing what comes out of these far-reaching experiments with seven welfare agreement municipalities, so that we can use the experience to improve welfare across all municipalities, and then we can talk about involving more municipalities and welfare areas. So, on this basis, we cannot support the proposal.

That is why the SF has taken a positive view of the experimental system of free municipalities and appreciates the motion for a resolution tabled by the Left. In SF we want to set the municipalities more free. Too much top management of the work in the municipalities involves cash thinking and inflexible solutions which are a nuisance to the citizens and the employees who carry it out. The experiments so far in the social, health and employment fields have shown that municipalities are very much able to handle their tasks better and cheaper by organising their own solutions. In the field of employment, fewer procedural requirements include: The meeting with the citizen and the opportunity to launch new initiatives as psychological assistance improved the results of the job centres, and at the same time it has increased the satisfaction of citizens and other stakeholders such as companies, teachers and cashiers, and this is what happens when municipalities are freed from top management and bureaucracy. That is why I also look forward to working on the welfare agreements together with seven selected municipalities in the area of the elderly, day care and school, and I also think it is very exciting if we provide opportunities for the schools in particular to be freer from the many general demands that are here from Christiansborg. Already there are exciting experiments in Holbæk and Esbjerg, these are two independent cities and not put together and we believe that further attempts at the liberation of primary schools could improve the school day for both teachers and pupils in many places in the country. We already have an agreement on this, and we will continue to do so in the autumn, which is why we in the SF do not believe that the school area should be taken in a future round according to this proposal. However, I can see that something similar could apply in other welfare areas, such as employment, as I have already mentioned, but also in the cultural field, I could imagine that greater freedom in the task solution could allow for new and creative local cultural opportunities in the municipalities. In the field of culture, we have already started work in the SF with the freedom of local representation on boards, and we must continue this liberation. However, we also need to be careful, because free municipalities must give more freedom away from rigid top management, but we must also ensure that this does not lead to a reduction in welfare or a violation of citizens' rights. It has previously been seen that experimental schemes can put citizens at a disadvantage, for example tenants in relation to their rights to live in a dwelling, and it can also help to reduce welfare or lead to increased privatisation, and it is clear that this is not something SF wants. In the SF we are open and tense to hear the municipalities' input on what they think could be good free municipalities attempts, but we also believe that the incoming proposals should be handled so that legal certainty and citizen-friendly welfare are not neglected. With the attention points we have with us, we look forward to working on this proposal and could help to free the municipalities, show them more confidence, while at the same time ensuring that welfare and security remain.

ParlaMint-DK_20210517160316	Thank you. We support the motion for a resolution tabled by the Left. In other words, all the positive things we see out there in free market players do not exist in politics. Actors in a free market can offer consumers what they want, and if they succeed, they will make very large profits, and if they do not succeed, they will go bankrupt. There is an experiment going on with how best to give consumers what they want. It is not the case that you can really get a different political system by moving from the City of Copenhagen to the Municipality of Frederiksberg. They are very similar, although there are differences. And it is, in fact, a mistake in politics that one cannot like experiment with going different ways so that one can see which way is best.
